What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Thank you for seeing me. When I zone out, my mind is just drifting to ordinary, everyday thoughts like what to make for dinner, rather than feeling disconnected from reality. It's really just a case of my attention wandering off to mundane things.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any episodes where you felt like you were in a daze or a trance, or had significant memory gaps for events that occurred, beyond just zoning out in meetings?\n\nPatient: No, I've never experienced any trances, dazes, or actual gaps in my memory. It's strictly that my focus slips and my mind drifts off to other topics during those long, dry meetings. I'm always fully aware of where I am, I just struggle to pay attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to sustain attention in other areas of your life, such as reading, watching TV, or during conversations with friends and family, or is this primarily a problem in work-related settings like meetings?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not just at work, although that's where it causes the most professional stress. At home, I really struggle to sit still and pay attention when watching a movie or trying to relax with my partner in the evenings. I get this wired, restless feeling inside that makes it hard to focus, and it's actually starting to strain our relationship."

It's strictly that my focus slips and my mind drifts off to other topics during those long, dry meetings. I'm always fully aware of where I am, I just struggle to pay attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to sustain attention in other areas of your life, such as reading, watching TV, or during conversations with friends and family, or is this primarily a problem in work-related settings like meetings?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not just at work, although that's where it causes the most professional stress. I get this wired, restless feeling inside that makes it hard to focus, and it's actually starting to strain our relationship.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself acting on impulse, doing things without much thought, like interrupting others, blurting out answers, or engaging in reckless behaviors?\n\nPatient: I definitely struggle with verbal impulsivity, though I don't do physically reckless things. In meetings, I often blurt out answers before someone has even finished asking because I think I know where they're going, which means I miss important details. I also catch myself interrupting colleagues, finishing their sentences, and jumping into conversations, only realizing afterward from their reactions that I've overstepped.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about this 'wired, restless feeling' you mentioned, such as whether it leads to fidgeting, an inability to sit still, or feeling constantly on the go?"

explaining the question, because as soon as I think I know where they’re going I blurt out my response and then realize I missed an important nuance at the end.\n- S018 (Interrupting_Intruding): I catch myself jumping into other people’s conversations in the office or finishing their sentences and redirecting the discussion, and only afterward do I notice the looks that say I’ve overstepped or What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Thank you for seeing me. I only realize afterward that I didn't actually hear what they were saying, and it's getting really embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happens when you 'zone out' - do you feel like you're still in the room but just not hearing, or do you feel like you're somewhere else entirely?\n\nPatient: I'm physically right there and usually looking directly at the person, but my mind has completely drifted off to mundane things, like planning what to make for dinner. Then I suddenly snap back and realize I didn't process a single word they said, leaving me completely unable to repeat it back.\n\nDoctor: When you 'zone out' and your mind drifts off, do you ever experience any feelings of detachment from yourself or your surroundings, as if you're observing things from outside your body?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel detached from my body or like I'm observing things from the outside at all. At home, I really struggle to sit still and pay attention when watching a movie or trying to relax with my partner in the evenings. I get this wired, restless feeling inside that makes it hard to focus, and it's actually starting to strain our relationship.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself acting on impulse, doing things without much thought, like interrupting others, blurting out answers, or engaging in reckless behaviors?\n\nPatient: I definitely struggle with verbal impulsivity, though I don't do physically reckless things. In meetings, I often blurt out answers before someone has even finished asking because I think I know where they're going, which means I miss important details. I also catch myself interrupting colleagues, finishing their sentences, and jumping into conversations, only realizing afterward from their reactions that I've overstepped.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about this 'wired, restless feeling' you mentioned, such as whether it leads to fidgeting, an inability to sit still, or feeling constantly on the go?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I sit still for more than 30 minutes, I get this wired, crawling feeling inside that makes me constantly jiggle my leg, click my pen, or find excuses to get up from my seat. It feels like I'm constantly on the go; my colleagues even joke about me pacing the halls, and I find it almost impossible to just sit and rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're driven by a motor, where you have to keep moving, and is this feeling present in both your work and personal life, making it hard for you to engage in leisure activities quietly?"

catch myself jumping into other people’s conversations in the office or finishing their sentences and redirecting the discussion, and only afterward do I notice the looks that say I’ve overstepped or derailed their train of thought.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- onset_prior_to_12_years: Even back in elementary school, teachers would write on my report cards that I was always out of my relationship because I’m often distracted and can’t just sit and relax with my partner in the evenings.\n- duration: These problems have really come to a head over the last couple of years since the Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Then I suddenly snap back and realize I didn't process a single word they said, leaving me completely unable to repeat it back.\n\nDoctor: When you 'zone out' and your mind drifts off, do you ever experience any feelings of detachment from yourself or your surroundings, as if you're observing things from outside your body?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel detached from my body or like I'm observing things from the outside at all. I only realize afterward that I didn't actually hear what they were saying, and it's getting really embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happens when you 'zone out' - do you feel like you're still in the room but just not hearing, or do you feel like you're somewhere else entirely?\n\nPatient: I'm physically right there and usually looking directly at the person, but my mind has completely drifted off to mundane things, like planning what to make for dinner. Then I suddenly snap back and realize I didn't process a single word they said, leaving me completely unable to repeat it back.\n\nDoctor: When you 'zone out' and your mind drifts off, do you ever experience any feelings of detachment from yourself or your surroundings, as if you're observing things from outside your body?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel detached from my body or like I'm observing things from the outside at all. When I zone out, my mind is just drifting to ordinary, everyday thoughts like what to make for dinner, rather than feeling disconnected from reality. It's really just a case of my attention wandering off to mundane things.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any episodes where you felt like you were in a daze or a trance, or had significant memory gaps for events that occurred, beyond just zoning out in meetings?\n\nPatient: No, I've never experienced any trances, dazes, or actual gaps in my memory. It's strictly that my focus slips and my mind drifts off to other topics during those long, dry meetings. I'm always fully aware of where I am, I just struggle to pay attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to sustain attention in other areas of your life, such as reading, watching TV, or during conversations with friends and family, or is this primarily a problem in work-related settings like meetings?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not just at work, although that's where it causes the most professional stress. At home, I really struggle to sit still and pay attention when watching a movie or trying to relax with my partner in the evenings. I get this wired, restless feeling inside that makes it hard to focus, and it's actually starting to strain our relationship.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself acting on impulse, doing things without much thought, like interrupting others, blurting out answers, or engaging in reckless behaviors?\n\nPatient: I definitely struggle with verbal impulsivity, though I don't do physically reckless things. In meetings, I often blurt out answers before someone has even finished asking because I think I know where they're going, which means I miss important details. I also catch myself interrupting colleagues, finishing their sentences, and jumping into conversations, only realizing afterward from their reactions that I've overstepped.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about this 'wired, restless feeling' you mentioned, such as whether it leads to fidgeting, an inability to sit still, or feeling constantly on the go?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I sit still for more than 30 minutes, I get this wired, crawling feeling inside that makes me constantly jiggle my leg, click my pen, or find excuses to get up from my seat. It feels like I'm constantly on the go; my colleagues even joke about me pacing the halls, and I find it almost impossible to just sit and rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're driven by a motor, where you have to keep moving, and is this feeling present in both your work and personal life, making it hard for you to engage in leisure activities quietly?\n\nPatient: Yes, I constantly feel like I'm driven by a motor. At work, colleagues joke that I can't sit still because I'm always pacing and starting new tasks, and at home, I end up reorganizing things instead of resting. It makes quiet activities like watching a movie with my partner feel almost unbearable, giving me a wired, crawling feeling that is really straining our relationship because I can't just relax.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often these symptoms of inattention, impulsivity, and restlessness occur, and have you experienced them consistently across your life, or have they developed more recently?\n\nPatient: While these issues have really come to a head over the last two years since my company's restructuring forced me into this meeting-heavy role, they aren't entirely new. Looking back, my elementary school teachers always wrote on my report cards that I couldn't sit still, talked over classmates, and was constantly sent to the hallway, even though I was smart and did well on tests.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any patterns or triggers that make your symptoms of inattention, impulsivity, and restlessness better or worse, such as time of day, environment, or specific situations?\n\nPatient: They are definitely much worse during long, back-to-back meetings, structured presentations, or budget reviews where I'm required to sit still for more than 30 minutes. Conversely, I do much better with dynamic, project-based work and quiet analysis where I can move around freely and organize tasks around my own rhythms.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your teachers noted these behaviors in elementary school; did you experience significant difficulties with attention, impulsivity, or restlessness during adolescence and young adulthood as well, or did these issues become more pronounced in recent years?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I'm not entirely sure, as I haven't thought about that period in my life very much. While I definitely had those issues in elementary school, I think I managed to get by through high school and college, and it wasn't until the recent major restructuring at work that things became truly difficult.

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Thank you for seeing me. I've been struggling a lot at work lately because in long meetings, people will talk directly to me and I just completely zone out. I only realize afterward that I didn't actually hear what they were saying, and it's getting really embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happens when you 'zone out' - do you feel like you're still in the room but just not hearing, or do you feel like you're somewhere else entirely?\n\nPatient: I'm physically right there and usually looking directly at the person, but my mind has completely drifted off to mundane things, like planning what to make for dinner. Then I suddenly snap back and realize I didn't process a single word they said, leaving me completely unable to repeat it back.\n\nDoctor: When you 'zone out' and your mind drifts off, do you ever experience any feelings of detachment from yourself or your surroundings, as if you're observing things from outside your body?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel detached from my body or like I'm observing things from the outside at all. When I zone out, my mind is just drifting to ordinary, everyday thoughts like what to make for dinner, rather than feeling disconnected from reality. It's really just a case of my attention wandering off to mundane things.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any episodes where you felt like you were in a daze or a trance, or had significant memory gaps for events that occurred, beyond just zoning out in meetings?\n\nPatient: No, I've never experienced any trances, dazes, or actual gaps in my memory. It's strictly that my focus slips and my mind drifts off to other topics during those long, dry meetings. I'm always fully aware of where I am, I just struggle to pay attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to sustain attention in other areas of your life, such as reading, watching TV, or during conversations with friends and family, or is this primarily a problem in work-related settings like meetings?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not just at work, although that's where it causes the most professional stress. At home, I really struggle to sit still and pay attention when watching a movie or trying to relax with my partner in the evenings. I get this wired, restless feeling inside that makes it hard to focus, and it's actually starting to strain our relationship.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself acting on impulse, doing things without much thought, like interrupting others, blurting out answers, or engaging in reckless behaviors?\n\nPatient: I definitely struggle with verbal impulsivity, though I don't do physically reckless things. I also catch myself interrupting colleagues, finishing their sentences, and jumping into conversations, only realizing afterward from their reactions that I've overstepped.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about this 'wired, restless feeling' you mentioned, such as whether it leads to fidgeting, an inability to sit still, or feeling constantly on the go?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I sit still for more than 30 minutes, I get this wired, crawling feeling inside that makes me constantly jiggle my leg, click my pen, or find excuses to get up from my seat. It feels like I'm constantly on the go; my colleagues even joke about me pacing the halls, and I find it almost impossible to just sit and rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're driven by a motor, where you have to keep moving, and is this feeling present in both your work and personal life, making it hard for you to engage in leisure activities quietly?\n\nPatient: Yes, I constantly feel like I'm driven by a motor. At work, colleagues joke that I can't sit still because I'm always pacing and starting new tasks, and at home, I end up reorganizing things instead of resting. It makes quiet activities like watching a movie with my partner feel almost unbearable, giving me a wired, crawling feeling that is really straining our relationship because I can't just relax.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often these symptoms of inattention, impulsivity, and restlessness occur, and have you experienced them consistently across your life, or have they developed more recently?\n\nPatient: While these issues have really come to a head over the last two years since my company's restructuring forced me into this meeting-heavy role, they aren't entirely new. Looking back, my elementary school teachers always wrote on my report cards that I couldn't sit still, talked over classmates, and was constantly sent to the hallway, even though I was smart and did well on tests.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any patterns or triggers that make your symptoms of inattention, impulsivity, and restlessness better or worse, such as time of day, environment, or specific situations?\n\nPatient: They are definitely much worse during long, back-to-back meetings, structured presentations, or budget reviews where I'm required to sit still for more than 30 minutes. Conversely, I do much better with dynamic, project-based work and quiet analysis where I can move around freely and organize tasks around my own rhythms.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your teachers noted these behaviors in elementary school; did you experience significant difficulties with attention, impulsivity, or restlessness during adolescence and young adulthood as well, or did these issues become more pronounced in recent years?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure, as I haven't thought about that period in my life very much. While I definitely had those issues in elementary school, I think I managed to get by through high school and college, and it wasn't until the recent major restructuring at work that things became truly difficult.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify whether you believe these symptoms of inattention, impulsivity, and restlessness have been present and caused significant impairment consistently throughout your life, or if they have significantly worsened in recent years, particularly in relation to the changes in your work environment?"

Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
